# Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes

Amrith Setlur Affiliation: Meta Affiliation: Carnegie Mellon University
Work done at Meta    Zijian Wang Affiliation: Meta    Andrew Cohen
Affiliation: Meta Work done at Meta    Paria Rashidinejad
Affiliation: Meta Affiliation: University of Southern California Work
done at Meta Authors jointly advised this work.    Sang Michael Xie
Affiliation: Meta Work done at Meta Authors jointly advised this work.

###### Abstract

Typical reinforcement learning (RL) methods for LLM reasoning waste
compute on hard problems, where correct _on-policy_ traces are rare,
policy gradients vanish, and learning stalls. To bootstrap more
efficient RL, we consider reusing old sampling FLOPs (from prior
inference or RL training) in the form of _off-policy_ traces. Standard
off-policy methods supervise against off-policy data, causing
instabilities during RL optimization. We introduce PrefixRL, where we
_condition_ on the prefix of successful off-policy traces and run
on-policy RL to complete them, side-stepping off-policy instabilities.
PrefixRL boosts the learning signal on hard problems by modulating the
difficulty of the problem through the off-policy prefix length. We prove
that the PrefixRL objective is not only consistent with the standard RL
objective but also more sample efficient. Empirically, we discover
back-generalization: training _only_ on prefixed problems generalizes to
_out-of-distribution_ unprefixed performance, with learned strategies
often differing from those in the prefix. In our experiments, we source
the off-policy traces by rejection sampling with the base model,
creating a self-improvement loop. On hard reasoning problems, PrefixRL
reaches the same training reward 2$`\times`$ faster than the strongest
baseline (SFT on off-policy data then RL), even after accounting for the
compute spent on the initial rejection sampling, and increases the final
reward by 3$`\times`$. The gains transfer to held-out benchmarks, and
PrefixRL is still effective when off-policy traces are derived from a
different model family, validating its flexibility in practical
settings.

## 1 Introduction

Reinforcement learning (RL) is the de facto method to boost large
language model (LLMs) reasoning, especially for math and coding ([An
et al., 2025](#bib.bib6); [Liu et al., 2025b](#bib.bib41); [Guo et al.,
2025](#bib.bib26)). Most successful RL recipes ([Ahmadian et al.,
2024](#bib.bib4); [Yu et al., 2025](#bib.bib70)) are _on-policy_: sample
multiple reasoning traces (rollouts) from the current model and derive
updates from correct (and incorrect) traces. This paradigm breaks down
on _hard problems_ with low pass@$`k`$ (e.g., pass@2k $`\approx`$ 0),
where the model rarely samples a correct trace. In this regime, learning
stalls as RL spends enormous amounts of sampling FLOPs without receiving
any learning signal, and RL rewards plateau.

In practice, we are rarely solving these problems for the first time
since earlier RL runs or inference on previous models may have spent
compute on the same (or similar) hard problems. The natural question now
is how to reuse this ever-growing dataset of _off-policy_ traces, which
often contains some correct traces even for very hard problems, in order
to guide the online RL policy towards higher-rewarding states and
accelerate on-policy RL.

A straightforward approach is to treat the off-policy traces as
supervision: perform supervised fine-tuning (a.k.a., mid-training or
continued pretraining) on the correct off-policy traces followed by
standard on-policy RL ([Wang et al., 2025d](#bib.bib66)). However, SFT
on a small set of correct traces can lead to memorization ([Chu et al.,
2025](#bib.bib15)) and entropy collapse, which hurts exploration during
subsequent RL ([Zhang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib74)). Alternatively, we can
use off-policy traces directly in RL via importance weighting, but this
is often unstable due to high-variance gradient estimates ([Liu et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib40); [Yan et al., 2025](#bib.bib67)). Both options use
off-policy traces as target supervision, and since these off-policy
traces are very low probability under the RL policy, this leads to
suboptimal RL optimization.

PrefixRL places the RL policy in higher-rewarding states, which boosts
the learning signal. Performance transfers from the prefixed to
no-prefix problems via a phenomenon we call back-generalization.

To avoid these pitfalls, we propose PrefixRL: run on-policy RL
_conditioned_ on prefixes of correct off-policy traces instead of
directly supervising on them
(Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).
_First_, we extract and fix a few off-policy prefixes and append them to
the original problem to create _prefixed problems_. _Second_, we run
on-policy RL on both _no-prefix_ (original) problems and prefixed
problems, where gradients are masked on the off-policy prefix. The
prefixes extracted from correct off-policy traces place the current RL
policy in states that are more likely to lead to a correct answer on
hard problems, reducing gradient variance and increasing the strength of
the learning signal.

PrefixRL is consistent with and more sample-efficient than standard RL.
However, it is not immediately clear what the effect on the bias is. In
Section [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"),
we prove that when the prefixes are correct and realizable in the model
class, (i) maximizers of the PrefixRL objective also maximize
performance on the standard RL objective; and (ii) since the prefixes
lessen the exploration burden, PrefixRL reduces suboptimality gap with
less samples compared to standard RL (by a factor of context length).
Overall, PrefixRL changes the on-policy RL objective by using off-policy
prefixes solely to guide exploration and unblock training on hard
problems.

Back-generalization. Beyond the theory, we empirically find an
additional phenomenon behind the gains in PrefixRL we call
_back-generalization_, where on-policy RL on _only_ prefixed problems
substantially boosts test performance on the original no-prefix
problems, which were never trained on. Beyond the generalization in the
face of train/test mismatch, back-generalization is distinctive for two
reasons. First, back-generalization is a type of generalization via
shared parameters because it alters the next-token distribution on
prefixes it was never trained on (impossible in the tabular RL setting).
Second, we find that _back-generalization can be even more powerful than
standard generalization_ in RL (transfer across related problems or
environments). We show this in an in-context learning setup, where we
run RL on problems prefixed with another problem and reasoning trace in
context. We find that training on a problem P1 conditioned on a related
problem P2 in context improves generalization from P1 to P2 considerably
more than directly running RL on the problem P1 (see
Section [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Which Prefixes Back-Generalize the Most in PrefixRL? Analysis via In-Context Learning ‣ 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).

PrefixRL can discover and learn strategies beyond what is provided in
the prefix. Interestingly, the model does not simply back-generalize by
imitating the off-policy prefix it is conditioned on. Through controlled
experiments, we find that PrefixRL is more compute-efficient than
standard RL at amplifying successful strategies and rejecting suboptimal
ones, _even_ when the suboptimal strategy is explicitly present in the
off-policy prefix. As a result of observing non-zero rewards (and
advantages) more often, we hypothesize that PrefixRL allows the model to
more quickly identify the flaws in the suboptimal strategy and use this
insight to find a better strategy (see
Section [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 PrefixRL can Discover New Strategies Beyond What is Present in the Prefixed Problem ‣ 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).

Figure 2: PrefixRL affords a self-improvement pipeline that recycles RL
flops on hard problems. We instantiate PrefixRL for self-improvement by
collecting a dataset of off-policy traces through large-scale rejection
sampling on the base LLM (distilled Llama3.1-8B). In FLOPs-matched
training, PrefixRL outperforms the strongest baseline (SFT on
rejection-sampled data + RL): 2$`\times`$ higher compute efficiency
(including rejection-sampling cost) and $`>`$45$`\%`$ (over 3$`\times`$
relative) higher final training accuracy on no-prefix training problems
_(left)_, with gains transferring to standardized evals such as AIME ’25
_(right)_.

PrefixRL improves both compute efficiency and final performance. In our
experiments, we instantiate PrefixRL in a _self-improvement setting_ by
collecting a dataset of off-policy traces through large-scale rejection
sampling on the base model. On hard problems in training, PrefixRL
improves compute-efficiency over the strongest mid-training baseline
(SFT on the off-policy data followed by on-policy RL) by $`2\times`$,
even when we account for the initial compute spent on collecting the
off-policy traces via rejection sampling, and training accuracy by
$`>45\%`$ (over 3$`\times`$ relative) on the original no-prefix problems
(Figure [2](#S1.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
(left)). These gains transfer to held-out benchmarks: for example, on
AIME ’25, PrefixRL improves pass@1 by 12% over the strongest
mid-training baseline in a compute-matched comparison. Finally, we find
that PrefixRL is still effective when off-policy prefixes are sourced
from Qwen3-4B-instruct while the RL policy is a distilled
Llama-3.1-8B-instruct. This setting provides similar compute and
accuracy gains, demonstrating the flexibility of PrefixRL to the
off-policy data source and model size.

## 2 Preliminaries

Setup. We use $`\mathbf{x}`$ to denote a problem and
$`\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{H})`$ for a response of $`H`$ tokens
sampled auto-regressively from an LLM or policy $`\pi`$ in policy class
$`\Pi`$, where $`\mathbf{y}_{:h}`$ refers to the prefix consisting of
first $`h`$ tokens in $`\mathbf{y}`$. We use $`\pi^{0}`$ to denote the
base (pre-trained) LLM that we want to post-train on a dataset of hard
problems $`\mathcal{D}=:\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{D}|}`$ with
verifiable rewards. For each $`\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}`$, we have an
outcome reward $`r(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y})`$ which is 1 when
$`\mathbf{y}`$ is correct and 0 when incorrect. We say $`\mathbf{x}`$ is
a _hard problem_ for policy $`\pi^{0}`$ if pass@512 under $`\pi^{0}`$ is
$`\approx 0`$ for $`\mathbf{x}`$. We use
$`J(\pi):=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\rho}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}\sim\pi(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x})}r(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})`$
to denote the performance of $`\pi`$ on $`\rho`$ which is the empirical
distribution over $`\cal{D}`$. For full set of notations, see
Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Full Set of Notations ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes").
Our goal is to train $`\pi^{0}`$ on $`\cal{D}`$, to maximize the return
$`J(\pi)`$ with access to previously spent inference compute on
$`\pi^{0}`$ (or on models fine-tuned from it), available in the form of
correct off-policy traces $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$.

Source of off-policy traces. To situate ourselves in a
_self-improvement_ setup, we mainly source the off-policy traces via
rejection sampling done on top of the base model $`\pi^{0}`$.
Concretely, for each $`\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}`$, we collect a single
correct trace by running rejection sampling on $`\pi^{0}`$ until we see
a correct trace. Therefore, if the pass@1 under $`\pi^{0}`$ is
$`p_{\mathbf{x}}`$ on $`\mathbf{x}`$, then in expectation we will sample
$`\nicefrac{{1}}{{p_{\mathbf{x}}}}`$ traces to get a correct one. Doing
this for every $`\mathbf{x}\in\cal{D}`$ gives us
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$, where
$`|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}|=|\cal{D}|`$. In theory, we assume that
the empirical distribution defined by $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ can
be _realized_ (perfectly fitted) by some $`\mu\in\Pi`$. In practice,
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ can also be curated with sophisticated
inference algorithms that may not be representable by using greater
compute depth than the architecture (by scaffolding sequential and
parallel compute) or having oracle access. We show the flexibility of
the off-policy source in
Section [5](#S5 "5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes").

RL algorithms. For RL that does not directly train on
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$, we use the REINFORCE ([Ahmadian et al.,
2024](#bib.bib4)) update. For others like importance-weighted off-policy
RL see CISPO update in
Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Implementation details for PrefixRL and Baselines ‣ Appendix E Additional Experiments and Details for Results in Section ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes").
Starting from $`\pi^{0}`$, REINFORCE iteratively updates $`\pi^{t}`$ by
ascending $`J(\pi)`$ using the gradient:
$`\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\rho}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}\sim\pi^{t}(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x})}\left[A_{\pi^{t}}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\cdot\log\;\pi^{t}(\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{x})\right]`$.
Following [Guo et al. (2025)](#bib.bib26), we sample $`n`$ i.i.d. traces
from $`\pi^{t}(\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{x})`$ to estimate the expectation
in the policy gradient, and use the group baseline
$`\hat{A}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{i})=r(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{i})-\tfrac{1}{n}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n}r(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{j})`$
for the advantage. On hard problems with pass@$`n\approx 0`$, all $`n`$
samples typically fail, so $`\hat{A}`$ (and the gradient) is usually
zero, yielding the _stalling regime_.

## 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes

![Refer to caption](2601.18795v2/prefixRL-fig.png)

Figure 3: Prefixing on off-policy trace improves probability of future
success. When we condition on prefixed problems, we increase the
accuracy by placing the policy at key strategy-revealing states (Erdös
Lemma in example

The accuracy is near zero until a key state is visited, after which it
jumps sharply.

In this section, we introduce the PrefixRL framework, which conditions
on prefixes from $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ to guide on-policy
exploration, boosting success on prefixed problems and transferring to
the original no-prefix problems. We also prove some theoretical
properties of the PrefixRL trained model. Before that, we discuss a more
direct approach first.

Directly training on $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$. Using
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ directly via (i) SFT (mid-training) or
(ii) importance-weighted off-policy RL ([Degris et al.,
2012](#bib.bib19)) can degrade exploration and stability in our setting.
Warm-starting the RL run with SFT can boost pass@1 but quickly collapses
entropy, leading to a drop in pass@64 after $`\sim`$100 RL iterations
(Figure [13](#A2.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ Appendix B Using Off-Policy Traces as Supervision Targets ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(a,b)
in
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Using Off-Policy Traces as Supervision Targets ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).
While early-stopping SFT may preserve randomness ([Wang et al.,
2025d](#bib.bib66)), for these issues to go away we would eventually
need a much larger $`|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}|`$ which would raise
upfront cost. Off-policy RL on $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ is often
unstable due to large-variance or clipped importance weights, yielding
biased gradients ([Agarwal et al., 2021](#bib.bib2)) and potential
reward collapse/memorization
(Figure [13](#A2.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ Appendix B Using Off-Policy Traces as Supervision Targets ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(c,d)) ([Kang
et al., 2024a](#bib.bib35); [Setlur et al., 2024](#bib.bib51)).

Creating prefixed problems in PrefixRL. Instead of imitating off-policy
traces, PrefixRL runs on-policy RL conditioned on off-policy prefixes
(in addition to the original no-prefix problems). Crucially, the
gradients are always masked on the off-policy prefix, avoiding the
instability of policy gradients on very off-policy tokens. We create a
dataset of prefixed problems $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{pre}}`$ by taking
prefixes from a correct trace
$`\mathbf{y}^{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ (for a problem
$`\mathbf{x}`$) and appending the first $`h`$ tokens
($`\mathbf{y}^{\mathbf{x}}_{1:h}`$) in $`\mathbf{y}^{\mathbf{x}}`$ to
the original problem $`\mathbf{x}`$, creating the prefixed problem
$`\mathrm{concat}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\mathbf{x}}_{1:h})`$ (see
Appendix [E.5](#A5.SS5 "E.5 Constructing Prefixed Problems: An Example ‣ Appendix E Additional Experiments and Details for Results in Section ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
for an example). We create multiple prefixed problems for every original
problem by choosing multiple values of the prefix lengths $`h`$. We
choose the values of $`h`$ such that, conditioned on the prefix, the
base LLM has a reasonable accuracy under base LLM (see
Section [5](#S5 "5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).
Typically these are states revealing a high-level problem-solving
strategy which the base LLM has little probability of sampling on its
own (see
Figure [3](#S3.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).

PrefixRL training objective. The PrefixRL objective in
([3.1](#S3.E1 "Equation 3.1 ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"))
optimizes rewards on both prefixed problems in
$`\cal{D}_{\mathrm{pre}}`$ and no-prefix ones in $`\mathcal{D}`$ within
a maximum context length of $`H`$ tokens. Note that the reward
$`r(\mathbf{x},\cdot)`$ for any prefixed problem
$`\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{pre}}`$ is identical to its no-prefix counterpart.

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |     |       |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --- | ----- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\textbf{{\color[rgb]{0.22,0.45,0.7}(PrefixRL)}}\quad\;\;\max_{\pi}\;\bigg(\underbrace{\sum_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{pre}}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{pre}}}\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}\sim\pi(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{pre}})}\left[r(\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{pre}},\mathbf{y})\right]}_{\textbf{prefixed problems}}\;\;+\;\;\underbrace{\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\cal{D}}\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}\sim\pi(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x})}\left[r(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right]}_{\textbf{{{{\color[rgb]{0.22,0.45,0.7}Standard RL}}}: No-Prefix Problems}}\bigg)`$ |     | (3.1) |

### 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees

Prefixing problems with partial correct traces should place the policy
in a higher-rewarding state
(Figure [3](#S3.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).
The central theoretical question, however, is not whether learning the
prefixed-problems are easier, but instead whether optimizing over such
_augmented_ problem sets provably improves the performance on the
_original_ RL objective $`J(\pi)`$. In general, training on an altered
input distribution could change the objective away from maximizing
$`J(\pi)`$.

We show that this is _not_ the case for PrefixRL as long as the prefixes
come from correct traces generated by a realizable policy. Concretely,
we prove: (i) objective consistency: every maximizer of the PrefixRL
objective is also a maximizer of $`J(\pi)`$; and (ii) sample complexity
guarantees and improvement over online RL: for a natural policy gradient
variant, PrefixRL achieves a smaller suboptimality bound, which
translates to a smaller number of on-policy samples required to reach a
given reward $`J(\pi)`$. In other words, we formally show that PrefixRL
reuses your FLOPs: it converts information already paid for in logged
prefixes into sample-complexity advantages over standard RL.

PrefixRL objective is consistent with standard RL. We make the following
assumption that the prefixes are taken from correct traces and that they
are realized by some policy $`\mu`$, i.e., there is a policy $`\mu`$
that can perfectly fit $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$.

###### Assumption 3.1 (Realizability and  correctness  of  off-policy  traces).

For any $`(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$: (i)
the trace is correct: $`r(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=1`$, and (ii) all
traces are realized by an optimal $`\mu`$, i.e., there exists an optimal
policy $`\mu\in\Pi`$ s.t. $`{\mu}(\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{x})=1`$.

The next theorem states that as long as prefixes are taken from the
correct traces realized by a policy $`\mu\in\Pi`$, optimizing the
PrefixRL objective preserves optimality on $`J(\pi)`$ (performance on
no-prefix problems only).

###### Theorem 3.2 (Consistency   of   the   PrefixRL   objective).

Assume the realizability and correctness of off-policy traces (Assm.
[3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Assumption 3.1 (Realizability and correctness of off-policy traces). ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).
Then, the maximizer of the PrefixRL
objective ([3.1](#S3.E1 "Equation 3.1 ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"))
also maximizes standard RL objective $`J(\pi)`$.

Intuitively, a good policy (yields high $`J(\pi)`$) on the no-prefix
problems should be able to complete its prefix counterparts and get the
same reward. This is because the prefixes in
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ are from correct traces and the
realizability assumption tells us that there should exist at least one
such good policy that can remain optimal on the prefixes in
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$; thus the two terms in the objective need
not be conflicting at optima. Note that while PrefixRL does not change
the global solution, it does not produce the same gradients as the
standard RL objective.

PrefixRL is more sample-efficient than standard RL. Having established
that PrefixRL does not bias policy optimization, we now quantify the
benefits of prefixing in terms of the number of on-policy samples needed
to reach a near-optimal policy. We analyze PrefixRL by instantiating the
policy update to be natural policy gradient ([Kakade, 2001](#bib.bib33))
(PrefixRL-NPG). See
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ C.2 Proof of Theorem ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
and discussion in
Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Proof of Theorem ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes").
Concretely, each RL iteration alternates between: (i) _policy
evaluation_ by fitting critic or $`Q`$-function $`\in\mathcal{F}`$ using
$`N`$ on-policy completions conditioned on the off-policy prefix, and
(ii) _policy improvement_ using the fitted critic via a mirror-descent
update. In practice, algorithms like PPO, GRPO approximate the NPG
update ([Schulman et al., 2017](#bib.bib50)).
Theorem [3.3](#S3.Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3.3 (Suboptimality gap of PrefixRL). ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
bounds the suboptimality of the policy $`\bar{\pi}_{T}`$ returned by
PrefixRL-NPG
(Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ C.2 Proof of Theorem ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"))
in terms of the number of policy updates $`T`$, on-policy completions
$`N`$, and a single distribution shift quantity between base policy
$`\pi_{0}`$ and policy $`\mu`$ that realizes
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$.

###### Theorem 3.3 (Suboptimality gap of PrefixRL).

Under
Assumption [3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Assumption 3.1 (Realizability and correctness of off-policy traces). ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"),
let $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ be realized by $`\mu\in\Pi`$. For any
$`\delta\in[0,1]`$, with probability at least $`1-\delta`$, policy
$`\bar{\pi}_{T}`$ returned by PrefixRL-NPG
(Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ C.2 Proof of Theorem ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"))
satisfies:

|     |                                                                                                                          |     |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --- | ---------------------------------------------------------- | ------- | --------------------------- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\max*{\pi\in\Pi}\;\;J(\pi)\;-\;J(\bar{\pi}*{T})\;\;\leq\;\;\;\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{KL}(\mu |     | \pi\_{0})}{T}}\;+\;\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\cdot\log\left(\frac{T | \cal{F} | }{\delta}\right)}\right).`$ |     |

The bound decomposes into (i) an _optimization_ term giving a
$`1/\sqrt{T}`$ convergence rate (with an oracle evaluator,
$`N\to\infty`$), whose constant depends only on the initial shift
between the base policy $`\pi_{0}`$ and the behavior policy $`\mu`$
realizing $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ and does not accumulate over
iterations; in particular, if $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ comes from
rejection sampling $`\pi_{0}`$ with at most $`R`$ attempts per problem,
then $`\mu=\pi_{0}`$ conditioned on success and
$`\mathrm{KL}(\mu||\pi_{0})=\mathcal{O}(\log R)`$ so this dependence
grows only logarithmically in $`R`$; and (ii) a _critic approximation_
term from fitting $`Q_{\pi_{t}}\in\mathcal{F}`$ using $`N`$ on-policy
traces, which in PrefixRL does not pay an extra distribution-shift
penalty because sampling and evaluation use the same reset distribution
induced by prefixes from $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ (unlike standard
RL, which still suffers mismatch relative to $`\pi^{\star}`$).

###### Proposition 3.4 (Worst-case separation with standard RL).

Let $`\bar{\pi}_{T}^{\mathrm{pre}}`$ be the policy returned after $`T`$
PrefixRL iterations of
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ C.2 Proof of Theorem ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
and $`\bar{\pi}_{T}^{\mathrm{std}}`$ be the policy after $`T`$
iterations of standard RL (states in
Algorithm [1](#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ C.2 Proof of Theorem ‣ Appendix C Omitted Proofs ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
$`\sim\pi_{t}`$). Then, there exists a reward function $`r`$ and base
LLM $`\pi^{0}`$ such that
$`J(\bar{\pi}_{T}^{\mathrm{pre}})-J(\bar{\pi}_{T}^{\mathrm{std}})\geq 1-\left(TN\cdot e^{-H}\right)`$
for $`TN=o(e^{H})`$.

Worst-case separation with standard RL.
Proposition [3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 3.4 (Worst-case separation with standard RL). ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
shows there exists a reward function and base LLM where on-policy NPG
and PrefixRL-NPG separate sharply. Intuitively, this happens when NPG is
bottlenecked by exponentially rare (in $`H`$) rewarding traces, whereas
PrefixRL-NPG only needs polynomial (in $`H`$) samples to learn the
sequence of optimal actions in reverse, starting from the longest prefix
to the shortest one. Here the exploration needed to learn optimal
actions on longer prefixes is outsourced to the off-policy prefix, and
by the time the policy is presented with the shorter prefixes it has
already learned to complete optimally on longer ones.

{subfigure}

\[t\]0.32 {subfigure}\[t\]0.32 {subfigure}\[t\]0.32

\(a\)

\(b\)

\(c\)

Figure 4: Back-generalization (train/test mismatch): We run RL _only_ on
prefixed problems where the prefix length of the off-policy trace lies
in the shaded interval. We evaluate different RL training checkpoints
across the full range of prefix lengths, including no-prefix problems
(0% prefixing). Training on longer prefixes improves performance on
shorter prefixes and can eventually improve no-prefix, indicating
back-generalization (a,b). When training uses very long prefixes only
there is a severe train/test mismatch, and back-generalization to
no-prefix problems takes more training steps (c).

## 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL

In
Section [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"),
we showed that PrefixRL is consistent with standard RL except more
sample-efficient. Now, we show that an empirical phenomenon we call
back-generalization is a strong source of the gains behind PrefixRL and
is unexplained by our theory. Back-generalization is defined as the
performance improvement on no-prefix problems when we train _only_ on
their prefixed counterparts. Next, we study this with controlled
experiments.

### 4.1 PrefixRL Improves No-Prefix Performance Even When Training Only on Prefixed-Problems

We run on-policy RL only on prefixed-problems where the prefix lengths
are distributed uniformly between a fixed band of token-length
percentiles of the full off-policy trace, but we evaluate accuracy
across the full spectrum of prefix lengths, including the no-prefix
endpoint (0% prefixing). In
Figure [4](#S3.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"),
we see generalization to no-prefix problems despite not having trained
on them. This transfer from prefixed to no-prefix problems is
particularly notable since the prefixes are highly unlikely under the
base policy. When the training mixture includes relatively short
prefixes, the mismatch is moderate
(Figure [4](#S3.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
(a,b)). In this case, performance increases first near the trained band
and then progressively improves for shorter prefixes, eventually lifting
no-prefix accuracy. When training is restricted to very long prefixes
(Figure [4](#S3.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(c)),
the train/test mismatch with no-prefix problems is severe and the
transfer is slower, but longer training (e.g., 800 steps) still yields
measurable no-prefix gains.

### 4.2 PrefixRL can Discover New Strategies Beyond What is Present in the Prefixed Problem

Clearly, back-generalization improves performance on unseen shorter
prefixes, but the mechanism behind this is unclear. To understand this
better, we create a simplified setup where we run RL on the prefixes of
a single problem:

Setup. On two hard problems, we run PrefixRL on the prefixes of a single
off-policy trace in $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ (prefixed on $`>`$40%
of the tokens in the trace). We use a keyword heuristic to extract a
salient “strategy” present in the off-policy prefix and track its
presence in (i) the prefixed-problem (PP) itself, (ii) the model’s
response when conditioned on that prefixed-problem (response for PP),
and (iii) the early part of the trace on the original problem (untrained
states in the model’s response).
Figure [5](#S4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 4.2 PrefixRL can Discover New Strategies Beyond What is Present in the Prefixed Problem ‣ 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
probes how strategy usage evolves when running PrefixRL, where the plots
in (a) correspond to one hard problem, and (b,c) correspond to a
different one. The prefix is sampled from a fixed pool, so frequency in
PP is constant (dashed line). In contrast, the response patterns change
over training and reveal:

{subfigure}

\[t\]0.3 {subfigure}\[t\]0.3 {subfigure}\[t\]0.3

\(a\)

\(b\)

\(c\)

Figure 5: Strong coupling between responses for prefixed and no-prefix
problems.: We train only on prefixed-problems (PP) and track the
frequency of a strategy-indicating keyword in: (i) PP derived from
off-policy trace, (ii) model’s response to PP, and (iii) and the early
part (prefixes or states not trained on) of the response to the
no-prefix (NP) problem. There is a tight coupling of the strategies
present in the responses for PP and NP (througout RL), yet not purely
imitative of the strategy explicitly provided in the prefixed-problem
itself: the model can learn new strategies or suppress prefixed ones
(e.g., Erdős–Gallai). The plots in (a) correspond to one hard problem,
and (b,c) correspond to a different one.

Strategy usage is strongly correlated between prefixed and no-prefix
responses. There is a tight coupling between strategy use on prefixed
and no-prefix responses, which is difficult to explain since no-prefix
problems are never trained on and many prefixed problems ($`>`$90% in
Figure [5](#S4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 4.2 PrefixRL can Discover New Strategies Beyond What is Present in the Prefixed Problem ‣ 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(a))
may not even contain the keyword. This suggests that PrefixRL updates
internal state representations shared across responses for the prefixed
and no-prefix problems. This further helps show that back-generalization
indeed arises from favorable function approximation in LLMs and is not
simply because solutions learned from solving the prefixed problem can
be “stitched” into the no-prefix version (more in
Appendix [D.1](#A4.SS1 "D.1 Back-generalization from prefixed to no-prefix problems goes beyond stitching. ‣ Appendix D Additional Experiment Details and Results on Back-Generalization ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")),
as hypothesized by prior work that prefixes on hints ([Zhang et al.,
2025c](#bib.bib75)).

PrefixRL can _unlearn_ strategies in the prefixed-problem and discover
new ones. In Figure
[5](#S4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 4.2 PrefixRL can Discover New Strategies Beyond What is Present in the Prefixed Problem ‣ 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(b),
we see that the policy at initialization uses the “Erdős–Gallai theorem”
close to 90% of the time on the prefixed-problems since $`>`$50% of the
prefixed-problems contain references to it. Throughout training, the
frequency of traces mentioning “Erdős–Gallai” decreases steadily on
responses to PP, indicating that PrefixRL can downweight suboptimal
strategies in the off-policy prefixes. For the same hard problem as the
one in
Figure [5](#S4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 4.2 PrefixRL can Discover New Strategies Beyond What is Present in the Prefixed Problem ‣ 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(b),
in
Figure [5](#S4.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 4.2 PrefixRL can Discover New Strategies Beyond What is Present in the Prefixed Problem ‣ 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(c)
we note that despite being conditioned on “Erdős–Gallai”, RL upweights
the rare ($`<`$2%) strategy at initialization (“Dirichlet Theorem”).
This reweighting also transfers to the model’s behavior on the no-prefix
problem.

Figure 6: Performance transfer via back-generalization can be stronger
than typical generalization in RL: When we prefix on the problem and
full solution trace of one (in-context) problem (P2), and run Prefix RL
to solve a different but related problem P3 — P2, we are able to improve
performance on both P2 and P3 individually, and the performance is much
higher compared to running RL either problem individually. The same
holds in the opposite direction, when we run PrefixRL on P2 — P3. We do
not see these gains when the in-context problem is unrelated in the case
of P1 and P3.

### 4.3 Which Prefixes Back-Generalize the Most in PrefixRL? Analysis via In-Context Learning

To study when back-generalization is effective, we analyze it in
_in-context learning_ setup. We run RL on problems prefixed with another
in-context problem, letting us cleanly ablate the relationship between
the off-policy prefix and the on-policy suffix based on how related the
in-context problem is to the problem being asked to solve.

Setup. We run RL on a given problem with an entirely different problem
(and its solution trace) in its context or prefix. We consider two
problem sets: (P1, P3) where P1 and P3 are unrelated problems, and (P2,
P3) where P2 and P3 are related and can be solved with the same high
level strategy (see
Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D Additional Experiment Details and Results on Back-Generalization ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
for details on P1, P2 and P3). E.g., when we say we run PrefixRL on P1
$`\mid`$ P3, this means that we prompt the model to solve the problem P1
with P3 along with P3’s solution trace in its context. All three
problems are hard for the base model, with $`<`$1% pass@32.

Back-generalization occurs when the prefix and suffix are sufficiently
related. From
Figure [6](#S4.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ 4.2 PrefixRL can Discover New Strategies Beyond What is Present in the Prefixed Problem ‣ 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"),
when the problems are related (P2 and P3), PrefixRL on P2 given P3
achieves 63% pass@4 on P2 and 60% pass@4 on the untrained in-context
problem P3. Running standard RL on P2 alone predictably improves the
pass@4 of P2 to 18% but the performance transfer to P3 is limited. In
contrast, PrefixRL on unrelated problems (P3 $`\mid`$ P1 or P1 $`\mid`$
P2) performs similarly to doing RL on just P3 and P1 respectively. This
suggests that _back-generalization is more effective when the components
of the prefixed problem are related_, and in the in-context learning
setting, back-generalization can be stronger than standard
generalization across the two related problems. This also sheds light on
a back-generalization mechanism in standard reasoning, where LLMs often
make $`k`$ attempts at the problem in a single trace. Since the internal
representations can be shared across related in-context problems so as
to enable back-generalization, this could also be the case across
attempts at a problem (see
Appendix [D.4](#A4.SS4 "D.4 Mental Model: Function Approximation and Back-Generalization. ‣ Appendix D Additional Experiment Details and Results on Back-Generalization ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
for a mental model).

## 5 Experiments and Results

{subfigure}

\[t\]0.42

{subfigure}

\[t\]0.42

Figure 7: Training compute matched evaluation of PrefixRL. Using
prefixed-problems sourced by rejection sampling the base model, we run
PrefixRL on the dataset of hard training problems (low pass rate under
the base model) and measure accuracy on the no-prefix training problems
for (Left) Llama-3.1-8b-instruct and (Right) Qwen-3-4b-instruct. Even
accounting for the initial compute spent on rejection sampling (shaded
blue region), PrefixRL improves compute-efficiency by 2$`\times`$ over
the strongest baseline with and an absolute gain of 45% with Llama and
30% with Qwen.

We evaluate PrefixRL on math reasoning benchmarks: in compute-matched
hard-problem training it beats RL and SFT+RL baselines, and these gains
transfer to standardized evals. We ablate off-policy data sources,
showing robustness to prefixes from different model families, and
analyze training dynamics versus off-policy RL.

Experimental setup. We experiment with two thinking models:
Llama-3.1-8B-instruct and Qwen3-4B-instruct. Since Llama-3.1-8B-instruct
is not a thinking model, we distill it on OpenThoughtsV3 before all
experiments, but still refer to it as Llama-3.1-8B  ([Guha et al.,
2025](#bib.bib25)). For training, we select 1k hard problems from
DAPO ([Yu et al., 2025](#bib.bib70)) and OMNI-MATH (levels 6-8) ([Gao
et al., 2024](#bib.bib23)), where pass@512 of Llama-3.1-8B is zero. We
compare against on-policy RL ([Ahmadian et al., 2024](#bib.bib4)) and
off-policy baselines that use $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$: SFT
(mid-training) on $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ followed by standard RL
(SFT+RL), importance-weighted off-policy RL ([Mahmood et al.,
2014](#bib.bib43)), and LUFFY ([Yan et al., 2025](#bib.bib67)). All
evaluation results, unless noted, are on no-prefix problems. See
Appendix [E](#A5 "Appendix E Additional Experiments and Details for Results in Section ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
for implementation details. Details on FLOPs accounting for our
compute-matched plots are in
Appendix [E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Evaluation Protocols and FLOPs accounting. ‣ Appendix E Additional Experiments and Details for Results in Section ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes").

Off-policy Dataset $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ and prefixed-problems.
For each base LLM, we produce $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ using
large-scale rejection sampling until there is one correct off-policy
trace per problem in $`\mathcal{D}`$. For each problem, we generate
three prefixed variants by cutting three prefixes from its off-policy
trace at random cut points chosen uniformly between 40% and 80% of the
tokens in the trace. The 3k prefixed and 1k no-prefix problems
constitute the training data for PrefixRL.

### 5.1 PrefixRL is More Compute-Efficient on Hard Problems Than Standard RL

PrefixRL is $`2\times`$ more compute-efficient and achieves higher
training accuracy.
Figure [7](#S5.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
shows that with the same compute, PrefixRL achieves higher accuracy on
no-prefix problems compared to baselines for Llama-3.1-8B ($`45\%`$
greater) and Qwen-3-4B ($`30\%`$ greater) respectively. Even after
accounting for the initial rejection-sampling cost, PrefixRL improves
compute-efficiency by roughly $`2\times`$ over the strongest baseline
(SFT+RL). In contrast, standard RL and SFT+RL only slowly improve
accuracy despite increasing the number of parallel samples per problem
$`n`$ from 8 to 64. Thus, PrefixRL effectively reallocates wasted
sampling FLOPs in standard RL towards productively improving training
rewards. Our iteration-matched plots
(Figure [9](#S5.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ 5.1 PrefixRL is More Compute-Efficient on Hard Problems Than Standard RL ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(c))
show that our baselines have stable training curves, and increasing
samples per problem $`n`$ unsurprisingly attains higher accuracy in the
iteration matched plot. Thus, PrefixRL’s gains are not explained by
degenerate baseline runs.

{subfigure}

\[t\]0.32 {subfigure}\[t\]0.32 {subfigure}\[t\]0.32

Figure 8: Held-out pass@$`k`$ of PrefixRL trained Llama models: We
measure pass@k (%) on AIME’25, HMMT’25 and 200 problems from
IMO-AnswerBench for our distilled Llama-3.1-8B (base model) finetuned
with standard on-policy RL, and PrefixRL (with and without off-policy
suffix injection). Note that IMO-AnswerBench is sampled from a similar
distribution to our training problems, but is still held-out. The
horizontal dashed line marks the base model’s pass@64.

PrefixRL improves both pass@1 and pass@$`k`$ on held-out benchmarks.
Figure [8](#S5.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ 5.1 PrefixRL is More Compute-Efficient on Hard Problems Than Standard RL ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
shows that across AIME’25, HMMT’25, and IMO-AnswerBench, PrefixRL
consistently improves pass@k (for $`k\leq 64`$) over the baselines by
over 10% absolute, including off-policy RL methods such as
importance-weighting (off-Policy RL) and LUFFY. The gains are visible
already at $`k{=}1`$ and widen as $`k`$ increases. This gain is notable
since we only train on hard problems, so there is substantial transfer
from the better training accuracy on hard problems to solving both easy
and hard problems. On AIME’25 with Llama-3.1-8B, PrefixRL improves
pass@1 from 38.2 to 61.3, a sizable absolute gain given the benchmark’s
difficulty. We observe a similar effect on HMMT’25, where pass@1
increases from 29.2 to 49.4. These small-$`k`$ improvements are
diagnostically important: they suggest that the model is more likely to
instantiate the right high-level plan earlier in the trajectory,
aligning with our “backward generalization” hypothesis
(Section [4](#S4 "4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).
As $`k`$ grows, the performance gap generally widens (e.g., on AIME’ 25:
$`+18`$ points at $`k{=}8`$ and $`+28`$ at $`k{=}64`$). This pattern
indicates that PrefixRL improves the diversity of the overall search
distribution: additional samples explore more promising subspaces rather
than repeating low-value trajectories. In other words, PrefixRL enhances
both the _mean_ performance (seen in $`k{=}1`$) and the _tail_ (seen as
$`k`$ increases) of the trace distribution.

PrefixRL increases the support of solvable problems over the course of
training. Now we ask how much of the gains in
Figures [7](#S5.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"), [8](#S5.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ 5.1 PrefixRL is More Compute-Efficient on Hard Problems Than Standard RL ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
come from “sharpening” the model’s output distribution on problems that
were already solvable with more samples (pass@$`k`$) versus an actual
expansion in the support of solvable problems.
Figure [9](#S5.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ 5.1 PrefixRL is More Compute-Efficient on Hard Problems Than Standard RL ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(b)
shows that PrefixRL not only improves pass@1 but also steadily improves
compute-matched pass@32, while competing baselines largely saturate over
the course of training. This suggests that PrefixRL increases the set of
problems with non-trivial success probability rather than only
converting pass@$`k`$ into higher pass@1.

Uniform improvement across training problems. The above pass@32 trend is
mirrored by the pass@1 histograms in
Figure [9](#S5.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ 5.1 PrefixRL is More Compute-Efficient on Hard Problems Than Standard RL ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(a):
vanilla RL improves a narrow subset of problems (likely those that were
lucky enough to see positive samples early in the training) and
stagnates elsewhere, while increasing $`n`$ helps by raising the chance
of observing reward across the batch. Prior work attributes such
non-uniform progress to training plateaus via *ray
interference* ([Schaul et al., 2019](#bib.bib49)). In contrast, PrefixRL
improves pass@1 on a broader fraction of problems by collecting reward
on prefixed versions and leveraging back-generalization to advance the
no-prefix tasks.

{subfigure}

\[t\]0.32 {subfigure}\[t\]0.32 {subfigure}\[t\]0.32

\(a\)

\(b\)

\(c\)

Figure 9: Pass@$`k`$ evolution and iteration-matched comparison on
training problems. (a) Uniform pass@1 improvement: By design, the base
LLM places all training problems in the pass@1 bin ¡0.1. After 200
iterations, PrefixRL yields the most uniform gains across problems,
while RL concentrates improvements on a small subset with rare
successes; increasing $`n`$ partially mitigates this. (b) _New problems
solved:_ compute-matched pass@32 plots indicate that PrefixRL steadily
expands the set of solvable problems rather than merely converting a
fixed pass@$`k`$ (for small $`k`$) into higher pass@1, whereas the
baselines largely saturate on pass@32. (c) _Fair baselines:_
iteration-matched reward curves confirm stable training across methods,
so the compute-matched gains are not explained by unstable or degenerate
baseline runs.

Figure 10: PrefixRL is still effective with off-policy prefixes from a
different model family. We train Llama3.1-8b-instruct on
prefixed-problems constructed using prefixes of rejection sampled traces
from the Qwen-3-4b-instruct (left). Interestingly, even though prefixes
are more out-of-distribution than those rejection sampled from
Llama3.1-8b itself, they are still equally effective in improving on
hard problems compared to when the prefixes are sampled from Llama3.1-8b
(olive green line). We also plot the performance on AIME when we train
Llama with prefixes from Qwen (right).

PrefixRL is still effective with off-policy prefixes from a different
model family. Up to now, we have used PrefixRL with prefixes from the
same base LLM that we ran RL with. In many practical settings, it is
easier to source off-policy prefixes from another model that is
substantially different in training data or architecture; for example,
from the open-source community. Motivated by this, we compare PrefixRL
with mid-training and standard RL when the off-policy data is sourced
from a policy (Qwen3-4b-instruct) that is different from the base LLM
(Llama3.1-8B-instruct) in
Figure [10](#S5.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ 5.1 PrefixRL is More Compute-Efficient on Hard Problems Than Standard RL ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes").
Despite the Qwen prefixes being more off-policy due to having a
different training dataset and architecture, they end up being similarly
effective on both train and test problems as off-policy prefixes from
the Llama base model we run RL on. Note that the Llama base model
required more initial compute for rejection sampling to collect the
off-policy prefixes, which accounts for most of the difference in the
curves. In
Appendix [E.3](#A5.SS3 "E.3 Qwen with Llama Prefixes ‣ Appendix E Additional Experiments and Details for Results in Section ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"),
we also find that the reverse direction is effective, where we use Llama
to generate off-policy prefixes for Qwen.

Figure 11: RL Training Dynamics. (Left) Compared to RL and PrefixRL,
supervised learning on off-policy data (SFT+RL) leads to much lower
token-level entropy during RL, which may hurt exploration. (Middle)
PrefixRL has much lower “all negative ratio”, or the number of prompts
with all zero rewards (zero advantage) during training. Note that the
ratio is measured only on problems without any prefixes. (Right) When we
look at the average response length on only no-prefix problems (green),
we see PrefixRL generates shorter responses, mostly because correct
solutions are usually shorter and standard RL has worse accuracy. Blue
shows the average length across all problems, which is lower due to
prefixing. This shortening is one way that PrefixRL reduces gradient
variance and gains on compute efficiency compared to standard RL.

### 5.2 Training Dynamics of PrefixRL are More Stable Compared to Typical Off-Policy Methods

In this section, we investigate RL training dynamics (like gradient
variance) underlying the instabilities of off-policy RL. We also discuss
the higher signal-to-noise ratio of policy gradients observed by
PrefixRL as a result of the off-policy prefixes of correct traces
placing the current RL policy in states with non-zero advantages.

Figure 12: PrefixRL signal-to-noise ratio and safely training on
off-policy suffixes. PrefixRL simultaneously has higher gradient norm
(left) and lower gradient variance (right) than both standard RL and
importance-weighted off-policy RL. For off-policy RL in particular, the
importance weighting causes the gradient variance to be much higher and
causes a gradient norm spike, which is a sign of training instability.
PrefixRL avoids these issues while still leveraging off-policy data.

PrefixRL leverages off-policy data while preserving entropy for RL
exploration. It is common practice to mid-train models on reasoning
traces from more capable models or its own traces from base model
inference or past RL runs ([Zelikman et al., 2022](#bib.bib72)). This is
typically done to prime the RL initialization and improve coverage over
high-reward regions. But this typically comes at the cost of the model’s
token-level entropy which impacts its ability to explore during RL.
Figure [11](#S5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ 5.1 PrefixRL is More Compute-Efficient on Hard Problems Than Standard RL ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
(left) shows the average token-level entropy of the model’s next-token
distributions during the RL run. Doing SFT on off-policy data causes the
entropy to dramatically decrease and this only continues to drop further
to values as low as $`0.01`$ during RL, suggesting that reward
maximization during RL is mostly just sharpening the distribution over
correct traces the base model can already sample (after running SFT). In
contrast, PrefixRL preserves most of the token-level entropy while still
making use of off-policy data.

Fewer all-negative batches on hard problems.
Figure [11](#S5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ 5.1 PrefixRL is More Compute-Efficient on Hard Problems Than Standard RL ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
(middle) plots the fraction of _all-negative_ problems (i.e., problems
in a training batch for which all $`n`$ on-policy traces on that problem
receive zero reward), measured _only_ over no-prefix problems. PrefixRL
consistently reduces this ratio relative to on-policy RL. This reduction
reveals an underlying shift in the unconditional policy: as training on
strategy-revealing states proceeds, the model becomes more likely to
_enter_ regions where non-zero advantages are attainable (either due to
the prefix revealing “useful” strategies that are further reinforced
with positive rewards, or revealing likely but incorrect strategies that
are quickly down-weighted and unlearned
(Section [4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 PrefixRL can Discover New Strategies Beyond What is Present in the Prefixed Problem ‣ 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes"))),
thereby breaking the stalling regime
(Section [2](#S2 "2 Preliminaries ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).

PrefixRL achieves better accuracies with fewer sampled tokens.
Figure [11](#S5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ 5.1 PrefixRL is More Compute-Efficient on Hard Problems Than Standard RL ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
(right) tracks the average sampled tokens per trace across all prompts
(with and without prefixes). On the no-prefix problems, PrefixRL
eventually maintains a lower sampled token budget per trace while
achieving higher reward rates, implying better _iteration efficiency_.
This is perhaps expected since correct traces are biased to be shorter
and qualitatively, once the model internalizes the strategy, it reaches
decisive steps earlier, which reduces “unproductive wandering” later in
the horizon. Moreover, since PrefixRL trains on 3:1 mixture of prefixed
to no-prefix problems, the average number of tokens sampled (across all
problem types) per batch is less than $`\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}`$ of the RL
run. As a result of short length RL, the gradient variance for PrefixRL
is much lower compared to standard RL, despite the PrefixRL not being
biased
(Section [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).

PrefixRL policy gradients have a higher signal-to-noise ratio.
Figure [12](#S5.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ 5.2 Training Dynamics of PrefixRL are More Stable Compared to Typical Off-Policy Methods ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
reports gradient norm and gradient standard deviation for PrefixRL,
off-policy RL, and standard RL. For off-policy RL, in addition to the
on-policy samples, we also compute the batch gradient using the correct
off-policy traces (in $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$) for the problems
in the batch, whenever the problem observes no correct on-policy sample
in the $`n`$ attempts. We correct the sampling policy mismatch via
importance weighting: while an unbiased sequence-level, unclipped
correction is possible ([Tan et al., 2025](#bib.bib61)), it has
prohibitive variance, so we follow prior work and use clipped
token-level weighting (biased) ([Liu et al., 2025a](#bib.bib40))
(Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Implementation details for PrefixRL and Baselines ‣ Appendix E Additional Experiments and Details for Results in Section ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).
We estimate the expected gradient norm and sampled-gradient standard
deviation by maintaining moving averages of coordinate-wise first/second
moments and summing across coordinates (equivalently, tracing the
gradient covariance;
Appendix [E.4](#A5.SS4 "E.4 Computing the gradient norm and standard deviation metrics ‣ Appendix E Additional Experiments and Details for Results in Section ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).
Off-policy RL exhibits noisy, biased gradients and even a norm spike
(instability). Because rejection-sampled traces are extremely unlikely
under $`\pi_{0}`$ and later $`\pi_{t}`$, token-level weights can be tiny
(e.g., $`10^{-3}`$), shrinking the expected gradient so its norm stays
comparable to on-policy RL until the spike
(Figure [12](#S5.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ 5.2 Training Dynamics of PrefixRL are More Stable Compared to Typical Off-Policy Methods ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
(left)). In contrast, PrefixRL samples fewer tokens (lower variance) and
its prefixes roll the policy into higher-advantage states (higher
expected norm), yielding simultaneously higher gradient norm and lower
variance
(Figure [12](#S5.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ 5.2 Training Dynamics of PrefixRL are More Stable Compared to Typical Off-Policy Methods ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
(right)) i.e., a better gradient signal-to-noise ratio.

## 6 Related Work and Discussion

Learning from off-policy LLM rollouts. When on-policy search stalls due
to over-sharpening or “over-thinking,” a common approach is to supervise
on human or oracle-provided traces ([Lightman et al., 2023](#bib.bib39);
[Corrado et al., 2024](#bib.bib16)), but teacher-driven methods inherit
the teacher’s capacity limit ([Agarwal et al., 2024](#bib.bib3)) and
often require reward shaping ([Yan et al., 2025](#bib.bib67)), entropy
control ([Wang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib63)), and heavy hyperparameter
tuning ([Zhang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib74)); moreover, for hard problems,
long model-compatible chains of thought are scarce and mismatches can
collapse response diversity ([Kang et al., 2024b](#bib.bib36)). When
off-policy data come from ”close enough” (in KL divergence) policies as
in Async RL, reuse becomes more efficient ([Fu et al.,
2025](#bib.bib20); [Khatri et al., 2025](#bib.bib37)), yet large
importance weights and high gradient variance pose instability
risks ([Agarwal et al., 2021](#bib.bib2)), so practical systems cap
behavior-policy staleness to only a few RL iterations ([Sheng et al.,
2024](#bib.bib54)). More recent works on use off-policy data ([Huang
et al., 2025](#bib.bib31); [Zhang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib73)) also use
the entire off-policy data or parts of it as imitation targets on
problems where on-policy correct rollouts are rare. Different from the
above, PrefixRL conditions on off-policy prefixes from long-thinking
traces, as opposed to training on them before or during RL. Thus,
instead of suffering from instabilities caused by using off-policy data
as direct targets
(Sections [3](#S3 "3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
and
 [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Training Dynamics of PrefixRL are More Stable Compared to Typical Off-Policy Methods ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")),
PrefixRL benefits from the old inference FLOPs spent on off-policy
traces indirectly via back-generalization.

Conditioning on hints to improve on-policy RL. The above concerns
motivate approaches that do not treat off-policy trajectories as
targets; related directions condition on subgoals or plans ([Hong
et al., 2025](#bib.bib30)), higher-level abstractions ([Qu et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib47)). A related line of work augments prompts with hints
or partially revealed human solutions to “guide” on-policy RL ([Chen
et al., 2025b](#bib.bib12); [Li et al., 2025](#bib.bib38); [Qu et al.,
2025a](#bib.bib46)). AdaBack ([Amani et al., 2025](#bib.bib5))
adaptively searches for the minimal hint that improves performance over
human-written solutions, but is hard to scale to long-context “thinking
models” and large datasets. Similarly, QuestA ([Li et al.,
2025](#bib.bib38)) uses answer-hinted prompts derived from human
solutions. In general, these methods are only feasible when we have
access to solution traces written by a human or a more capable teacher
model. In contrast, PrefixRL enables a self-improvement loop by not
relying on external sources and instead reusing compute from prior
models. Moreover, our work also analyzes the back-generalization
phenomenon that may be shared across these methods, showing that it
cannot be explained by some of the “stitching” arguments made in prior
work ([Zhang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib75)).

Resetting to off-policy states in RL. The idea of “resetting” current RL
policy to off-policy states is not new in RL ([Kakade,
2003](#bib.bib34); [Bagnell et al., 2003](#bib.bib7); [Nair et al.,
2018](#bib.bib44); [Salimans and Chen, 2018](#bib.bib48); [Yin et al.,
2022](#bib.bib69); [Uchendu et al., 2023](#bib.bib62); [Silver et al.,
2016a](#bib.bib55); [Silver et al., 2016b](#bib.bib56); [Agarwal et al.,
2019](#bib.bib1); [Daumé III and Marcu, 2005](#bib.bib17); [Daumé III
et al., 2009](#bib.bib18)). [Chang et al. (2024)](#bib.bib10) also
applied the resetting idea for post-training LLMs with human feedback.
While similar in principle, their work does not study computational
gains accounting for the initial compute spent on collecting off-policy
traces. In fact, in their case the data is human labeled whereas our
work lies more in a self-improvement setting. Our contribution is to
instantiate this perspective for RL of reasoning LLMs. We show that a
relatively small dataset of _correct_ off-policy traces is sufficient to
enable effective resets that make hard, low-pass-rate problems trainable
even when on-policy rollouts almost never succeed. We also show that
PrefixRL yields a strictly better allocation of compute, even after
accounting for the inference cost of collecting the off-policy traces.

Improving exploration on hard problems in LLM reasoning. Small models
fine-tuned with RL can outperform much larger base models ([Liu et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib41); [Luo et al., 2025](#bib.bib42)), largely by
reinforcing long chain-of-thought behaviors like self-correction ([Qu
et al., 2024](#bib.bib45)) and reflection ([Gandhi et al.,
2025](#bib.bib22)). Yet, without careful controls, RL often
under-explores and leaves hard instances underprobed; empirically this
appears as a drop in pass@$`k`$ versus the base model ([Yue et al.,
2025](#bib.bib71); [Zhao et al., 2025](#bib.bib76)). One response is to
regularize training to curb over-sharpening via intrinsic-motivation
bonuses ([Gao et al., 2025](#bib.bib24)), entropy ([Wang et al.,
2025b](#bib.bib64)), count-based signals ([Song et al.,
2025](#bib.bib60)), or objectives that directly optimize
pass@$`n`$ ([Chow et al., 2024](#bib.bib14); [Balashankar et al.,
2025](#bib.bib9)), but these still inherit sparse-reward limits and
depend on easy problems for signal ([He et al., 2024](#bib.bib28)). A
complementary thread ([Setlur et al., 2025b](#bib.bib53)) exploits
base-model asymmetries, e.g., the verification–generation gap ([Setlur
et al., 2025a](#bib.bib52); [Song et al., 2024](#bib.bib59)), and can
combine with negative-gradient dynamics to chain such asymmetries across
updates ([Zhu et al., 2025](#bib.bib77)); nevertheless, models often
“under-think” ([Wang et al., 2025c](#bib.bib65)), persisting with wrong
high-level plans despite more rollouts. In contrast, PrefixRL avoids
carefully tuned auxiliary exploration objectives by reshaping the
start-state distribution directly. Empirically, we do not observe the
pass@$`k`$ regressions often induced by over-sharpening or over
exploration with token-level entropy regularizers. In the worst case,
uninformative prefixes recover standard on-policy RL
(Section [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Which Prefixes Back-Generalize the Most in PrefixRL? Analysis via In-Context Learning ‣ 4 Back-Generalization Boosts the Learning Signal in PrefixRL ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).

## 7 Conclusion

PrefixRL is fundamentally different from typical methods that _imitate_
off-policy data, relying instead on back-generalization to incorporate
off-policy data as _conditioning_ context while keeping updates
on-policy. We expect the design space for such algorithms to be broad
since conditioning is highly flexible and the mechanisms (and full
potential) of back-generalization aren’t fully understood. Crucially,
back-generalization leverages the capabilities of the model itself to
incorporate off-policy feedback. Thus, harnessing back-generalization is
a crucial ingredient for bootstrapping prior compute and continuous
self-improvement.

## Appendix A Full Set of Notations

Markov decision process. We use $`\mathbf{x}`$ to denote an input
problem and $`\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{H})`$ for a response of $`H`$
tokens, and if $`\mathbf{y}\sim\pi(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x})`$, then
$`\mathbf{y}`$ is sampled auto-regressively from the LLM $`\pi`$ fed
with input $`\mathbf{x}`$. Each token in this response $`\mathbf{y}`$
belongs to a set of tokens or actions $`\mathcal{A}`$. The state
$`\mathbf{s}_{h}`$ at time step $`h`$ is given by
$`(\mathbf{x},y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{h})`$, where the initial state
$`\mathbf{s}_{0}`$ is just the problem $`\mathbf{x}`$. The set of states
across all time steps is denoted by the class $`\mathcal{S}`$. We use
$`d^{\pi}_{h}`$ to denote the distribution over states
$`\mathbf{s}_{h}`$ at time step $`h`$ by rolling out the policy $`\pi`$
auto-regressively for $`h`$ time steps. For compactness, we write the
trajectory-level log-likelihood
$`\log\pi(\mathbf{y}\mid\mathbf{x})=\sum_{h=1}^{|\mathbf{y}|}\log\pi(y_{h}\mid\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{<h})`$.
For each problem we have access to outcome reward function
$`r(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{y})`$ to check whether the final answer in
response $`\mathbf{y}`$ is correct/incorrect (1/0) for the problem
$`\mathbf{x}_{i}`$ (e.g., by matching the boxed answer in the end of
$`\mathbf{y}`$ for math problems).

Dataset of hard problems and off-policy traces. We use $`\cal{D}`$ to
denote a dataset of $`N`$ hard problems
$`\mathcal{D}=:\{\mathbf{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}`$. We use $`\pi^{0}`$ to
denote the base pre-trained LLM that we initialize the RL algorithm,
$`\pi^{t}`$ as the policy after $`t`$ RL iterations and
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ as the dataset of off-policy traces.
Finally, we define the pass rate @$`k`$ for problem $`\mathbf{x}`$ and
LLM $`\pi`$ as
$`\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k}\sim\pi(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x})}\max(\{r(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k})`$.
In the main paper, we define the set of hard problems as those with
pass@$`k`$$`\approx`$$`0`$ under the base LLM $`\pi^{0}`$. See
Section [5](#S5 "5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
for how we select these low pass rate hard problems for training.

## Appendix B Using Off-Policy Traces as Supervision Targets

Figure 13: Supervising the policy on $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ can
cause diversity collapse or training instabilities during RL: (a, b)
Warm-starting the RL run by running SFT on
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ before (mid-training) reduces token
entropy (a) and hurts exploration during RL (worse Pass@64 performance
in (b)). (c, d) Directly using $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ during
online RL by updating the current RL policy importance-weighted
off-policy traces (in addition to on-policy traces) leads to training
instabilities. We see the gradient norm (clipped at 1.0) blow up during
training (d) and this leads to optimization collapse (c).

SFT on $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ can hurt exploration during RL. A
common way to improve RL on hard problems is to first mid-train (SFT) on
traces in $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ to warm-start RL training.
However, while SFT boosts post-RL pass@1, pass@64 drops after about 100
iterations
(Figure [13](#A2.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ Appendix B Using Off-Policy Traces as Supervision Targets ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(a)).
This loss of diversity is driven by a sharp entropy collapse after SFT,
which decreases further during RL
(Figure [13](#A2.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ Appendix B Using Off-Policy Traces as Supervision Targets ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(b)).
This suggests that here, RL mainly sharpens the distribution of
responses the model can already produce after SFT. In contrast, an
early-stopped SFT checkpoint can underfit and encourage random
exploration during RL \[[Wang et al., 2025d](#bib.bib66)\]. One can
partially mitigate this by enlarging the SFT dataset
($`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$), but doing so increases the upfront
cost of the SFT step.

Off-policy RL using $`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ leads to training
instabilities. A more direct way to use the off-policy data
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ is to do importance-weighted off-policy
RL \[[Degris et al., 2012](#bib.bib19)\] (see
Appendix [E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Implementation details for PrefixRL and Baselines ‣ Appendix E Additional Experiments and Details for Results in Section ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")),
which accounts for the distribution shift between the current RL and
sampling (rejection sampling on base LLM) policies. However, this
suffers from large gradient variance or heavily biased gradients due to
clipping and token-level weighting rather than sequence-level \[[Agarwal
et al., 2021](#bib.bib2)\]
(Section [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Training Dynamics of PrefixRL are More Stable Compared to Typical Off-Policy Methods ‣ 5 Experiments and Results ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")).
This can cause training reward collapse and unstable optimization
(Figures [13](#A2.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ Appendix B Using Off-Policy Traces as Supervision Targets ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")(c),(d))
as we force updates on very unlikely token sequences under the current
RL policy, leading to memorization of
$`\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$ \[[Kang et al., 2024a](#bib.bib35),
[Setlur et al., 2024](#bib.bib51)\].

## Appendix C Omitted Proofs

In this section, we present proofs for our theoretical results in
Section [3.1](#S3.SS1 "3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes").
We begin with the proof for
Theorem [3.2](#S3.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 3.2 (Consistency of the PrefixRL objective). ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
which implies that the PrefixRL objective is consistent with standard
RL, and any solution for our PrefixRL objective, is also a mazimizer of
the standard RL objective which just maximizes $`J(\pi)`$. Following
this, we show the proof for
Theorem [3.3](#S3.Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3.3 (Suboptimality gap of PrefixRL). ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
which bounds the suboptimality gap of an algorithm using natural policy
gradient (NPG) to optimize the PrefixRL objective. Note that this is
slightly different from the policy gradients we use in practice, but is
nevertheless insightful in informing a formal mental model for the gains
behind PrefixRL. We also provide a proof for
Proposition [3.4](#S3.Thmtheorem4 "Proposition 3.4 (Worst-case separation with standard RL). ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
that lower bounds the performance gap between PrefixRL and standard RL
in the worst case. In the end we list auxiliary lemmas useful for
analysis.

### C.1 Proof of Theorem [3.2](#S3.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 3.2 (Consistency of the PrefixRL objective). ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")

Let the standard (no-prefix) RL objective be

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                    |     |       |
| --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ----- |
|     | $`\displaystyle J(\pi)\;=\;\sum_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}}\;\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{y}\sim\pi(\cdot\mid\mathbf{x})}\bigl[r(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\bigr],\qquad J^{\star}\;=\;\max_{\pi\in\Pi}J(\pi).`$ |     | (C.1) |

For each $`\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{D}`$,
Assumption [3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Assumption 3.1 (Realizability and correctness of off-policy traces). ‣ 3.1 PrefixRL Objective-Consistency and Sample-Efficiency Guarantees ‣ 3 PrefixRL: On-Policy RL on Very Off-policy Prefixes ‣ Reuse your FLOPs: Scaling RL on Hard Problems by Conditioning on Very Off-Policy Prefixes")
gives a single correct trace $`\mathbf{y}^{\mathbf{x}}`$ such that
$`(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}^{\mathbf{x}})\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{off}}`$.
Given any cut index $`h`$, define the prefixed problem

|     |                                                                                                             |     |       |
| --- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ----- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{pre}}\;=\;\mathrm{concat}(\mathbf{x},(\mathbf{y}^{\mathbf{x}})_{1:h}),`$ |     | (C.2) |
